[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t get past this feeling that I’m dangerous, honestly. Like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, but someone who shouldn’t be trusted. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it feels like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse.\n\nAnd I know people might say, “near-misses happen,” or that it was caught, but it doesn’t feel like that in my head. It feels like I’m a disappointment—to the people I work with, to patients, to my family, to everyone who thought I could do this. I keep thinking everyone would be better off without me around or involved, because I’m just going to let people down or hurt someone. That’s kind of why I’m here. I don’t know how to stop feeling that way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I think. About a year and two months now. It started after I graduated high school, when I began taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I thought it was just stress from trying to do everything and prove I could handle it, but it just kind of stayed and got more tied into how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things that helped me feel like myself again after class or after a shift. I’d go jogging to clear my head, or I’d knit while watching something, or cook something kind of involved just because it made me feel calm. I used to read too, like actual books after class, not just staring at pages.\n\nNow I’ll come home and I’m still in my scrubs, and I’ll just sit on my bed for… I don’t even know how long. I think about doing those things, like I can see my running shoes or my knitting stuff right there, but it’s like I can’t make myself care enough to start. And even when I try, it just feels pointless or empty, so I stop. It’s not that I don’t remember liking them. I do. I just don’t feel it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t have, like, a plan to hurt myself, and I haven’t sat down and figured out some specific way or anything like that. But the thoughts are there more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there at night or getting ready for work and I’ll think, it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not in a dramatic way, I guess, more like this exhausted thought that if I could just stop existing for a while, or disappear, then I wouldn’t have to keep worrying that I’m going to mess something up again. Or disappoint everyone again. Sometimes it feels like people would be safer or less burdened if I just wasn’t around to cause problems.\n\nI know that sounds bad when I say it out loud. I don’t want to scare anyone, and I’m not saying I’m about to do something. It’s just… those thoughts keep coming up, and they feel really hard to push away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just don't feel pleasure from them even if you try to do them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but maybe it starts with not being able to care enough to even begin. Like, I used to have this whole routine in my head after class or work — I’d go for a jog to clear everything out, or I’d knit because it kept my hands busy and felt calming, or I’d cook something that took a little effort because that used to feel satisfying. Or I’d read, just to be somewhere else for a while.\n\nNow I’ll come home still in my scrubs and just sit on my bed. And I can see the things right there, like my running shoes or my knitting stuff, and I’ll think, I should do that, it used to help. But it feels like there’s no pull toward it anymore. Like I can’t make myself care enough to get up.\n\nAnd if I do try, it’s not really enjoyable. I’m just going through the motions and waiting for it to feel like it used to, and it doesn’t. So then I stop, and I end up back on my bed feeling stupid for even trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past year where your feelings of worthlessness and guilt were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, but it’s hard to compare it neatly, because right now it feels like it’s kind of the worst it’s been. Or at least the most stuck I’ve felt in it.\n\nThe reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps replaying in my head, and it feels like proof, somehow, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. I know maybe people would say, you know, everyone makes mistakes or it was caught, but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like this big flashing sign that I shouldn’t be trusted, and that I’m going to hurt someone if I keep trying to do this.\n\nAnd then it spreads out from there, like it’s not just about work. It becomes this feeling that I disappoint everyone, that I’m a burden, that people would be better off if I wasn’t around making things harder or making them worry. I don’t know if there were earlier periods in the year where it was worse than now… I think there were times it was bad, but this is the point where I couldn’t really keep pretending it was normal guilt anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness and guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most of the day, honestly. I wish I could say it just comes in waves and then I’m okay in between, but it’s more like it’s sitting in the background all the time, and then certain things make it louder.\n\nLike with work, or even just thinking about work, that near-miss keeps coming back into my head. I keep replaying it and thinking, if that happened once, what does that say about me? And then it turns into this whole thing where I’m convinced I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted with people. Even if I’m trying to do something else, it’s like part of me is still stuck on that thought.\n\nAnd then it spreads beyond that. It’s not only “I made a mistake,” it becomes “I am a mistake,” or “I’m a disappointment,” or “everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me.” So… yeah, it’s there for most of the day. Sometimes it eases up a little if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for more than two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been more than two years like this, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad about myself before, like I think most people do, but not this constant, heavy thing where it feels like it proves something about who I am.\n\nThis really became something I couldn’t get away from after that near-miss. Since then I just keep thinking I’m a danger, like I shouldn’t be trusted, like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I feel like I’m just a disappointment in general, not just at work. Like everyone would be better off without having to deal with me.\n\nSo… no, not for two years, not in this way. This feels more recent, but it’s been really intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel worthless or guilty at all?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I might have moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking the words, like “I’m worthless” or “I’m guilty,” but I don’t really have whole days where it’s just gone.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always there underneath. The reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. That near-miss keeps turning into this evidence in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, like I shouldn’t be trusted. And then it doesn’t stay contained to that one thing. It becomes this bigger feeling that I’m just failing people in general, and that everyone would be better off without me around or without me causing problems.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I get days free from it. Maybe brief stretches where I’m not focusing on it as much, but it comes back really easily.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any variation in the intensity of your feelings of worthlessness and guilt over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is some variation, but it never feels like it fully lifts, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume changes.\n\nWhen something reminds me of the near-miss, or when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. Then it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it becomes this whole spiral of, “I’m dangerous,” and “I shouldn’t be trusted,” and “I’m not meant to be a nurse.” It feels like that one incident is proof of something terrible about me, even though part of me knows people might say it doesn’t mean that.\n\nAnd then at other times, if I’m distracted or doing something else, it can fade into the background a little. But it’s still there. It’s like I’m carrying around this constant feeling that I’m a disappointment, and that everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about me. So the intensity changes, yes, but the feeling itself doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific events or situations that trigger your feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest trigger is anything that brings me back to that near-miss at work. Even if it’s something small, like thinking about being on the floor, or imagining having to make another judgment call, it just sets off this whole chain in my head.\n\nIt starts with, “I made a mistake,” but it doesn’t stay that simple. It turns into, “I’m dangerous,” like I can’t be trusted with people’s care, and then, “I shouldn’t be a nurse at all.” And once I’m there, it kind of spreads beyond work. I start thinking I’m not just bad at my job or unsafe at my job, but that I’m a disappointment as a person.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels really real when it happens. Like that one incident proves something about me. And then I start thinking everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about what I might mess up next. So yes, anything connected to that near-miss or being a nurse or being responsible for someone can make the guilt and worthlessness get really loud.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That near-miss keeps turning into this evidence in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, like I shouldn’t be trusted. And then it doesn’t stay contained to that one thing. It becomes this bigger feeling that I’m just failing people in general, and that everyone would be better off without me around or without me causing problems.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I get days free from it. Maybe brief stretches where I’m not focusing on it as much, but it comes back really easily.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any variation in the intensity of your feelings of worthlessness and guilt over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is some variation, but it never feels like it fully lifts, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume changes.\n\nWhen something reminds me of the near-miss, or when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. Then it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it becomes this whole spiral of, “I’m dangerous,” and “I shouldn’t be trusted,” and “I’m not meant to be a nurse.” It feels like that one incident is proof of something terrible about me, even though part of me knows people might say it doesn’t mean that.\n\nAnd then at other times, if I’m distracted or doing something else, it can fade into the background a little. But it’s still there. It’s like I’m carrying around this constant feeling that I’m a disappointment, and that everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about me. So the intensity changes, yes, but the feeling itself doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific events or situations that trigger your feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest trigger is anything that brings me back to that near-miss at work. Even if it’s something small, like thinking about being on the floor, or imagining having to make another judgment call, it just sets off this whole chain in my head.\n\nIt starts with, “I made a mistake,” but it doesn’t stay that simple. It turns into, “I’m dangerous,” like I can’t be trusted with people’s care, and then, “I shouldn’t be a nurse at all.” And once I’m there, it kind of spreads beyond work. I start thinking I’m not just bad at my job or unsafe at my job, but that I’m a disappointment as a person.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels really real when it happens. Like that one incident proves something about me. And then I start thinking everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about what I might mess up next. So yes, anything connected to that near-miss or being a nurse or being responsible for someone can make the guilt and worthlessness get really loud.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. Even small decisions can feel weirdly hard, like what to chart first or what task to start with. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

No… not completely absent. I know I kind of already said that, but it’s hard to explain because sometimes it isn’t as loud every second, so part of me wants to say, “Maybe there were breaks,” but when I really think about it, it’s still there.

Like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, but someone who shouldn’t be trusted. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it feels like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse.\n\nAnd I know people might say, “near-misses happen,” or that it was caught, but it doesn’t feel like that in my head. I just don’t feel it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t have, like, a plan to hurt myself, and I haven’t sat down and figured out some specific way or anything like that. But the thoughts are there more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there at night or getting ready for work and I’ll think, it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not in a dramatic way, I guess, more like this exhausted thought that if I could just stop existing for a while, or disappear, then I wouldn’t have to keep worrying that I’m going to mess something up again. Or disappoint everyone again. Sometimes it feels like people would be safer or less burdened if I just wasn’t around to cause problems.\n\nI know that sounds bad when I say it out loud. I don’t want to scare anyone, and I’m not saying I’m about to do something. It’s just… those thoughts keep coming up, and they feel really hard to push away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just don't feel pleasure from them even if you try to do them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but maybe it starts with not being able to care enough to even begin. Like, I used to have this whole routine in my head after class or work — I’d go for a jog to clear everything out, or I’d knit because it kept my hands busy and felt calming, or I’d cook something that took a little effort because that used to feel satisfying. Or I’d read, just to be somewhere else for a while.\n\nNow I’ll come home still in my scrubs and just sit on my bed. Like I can’t make myself care enough to get up.\n\nAnd if I do try, it’s not really enjoyable. I’m just going through the motions and waiting for it to feel like it used to, and it doesn’t. So then I stop, and I end up back on my bed feeling stupid for even trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past year where your feelings of worthlessness and guilt were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, but it’s hard to compare it neatly, because right now it feels like it’s kind of the worst it’s been. Or at least the most stuck I’ve felt in it.\n\nThe reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps replaying in my head, and it feels like proof, somehow, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. I know maybe people would say, you know, everyone makes mistakes or it was caught, but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like this big flashing sign that I shouldn’t be trusted, and that I’m going to hurt someone if I keep trying to do this.\n\nAnd then it spreads out from there, like it’s not just about work. It becomes this feeling that I disappoint everyone, that I’m a burden, that people would be better off if I wasn’t around making things harder or making them worry. I don’t know if there were earlier periods in the year where it was worse than now… I think there were times it was bad, but this is the point where I couldn’t really keep pretending it was normal guilt anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness and guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most of the day, honestly. I wish I could say it just comes in waves and then I’m okay in between, but it’s more like it’s sitting in the background all the time, and then certain things make it louder.\n\nLike with work, or even just thinking about work, that near-miss keeps coming back into my head. I keep replaying it and thinking, if that happened once, what does that say about me? And then it turns into this whole thing where I’m convinced I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted with people. Even if I’m trying to do something else, it’s like part of me is still stuck on that thought.\n\nAnd then it spreads beyond that. It’s not only “I made a mistake,” it becomes “I am a mistake,” or “I’m a disappointment,” or “everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me.” So… yeah, it’s there for most of the day. Sometimes it eases up a little if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for more than two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been more than two years like this, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad about myself before, like I think most people do, but not this constant, heavy thing where it feels like it proves something about who I am.\n\nThis really became something I couldn’t get away from after that near-miss. Since then I just keep thinking I’m a danger, like I shouldn’t be trusted, like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I feel like I’m just a disappointment in general, not just at work. Like everyone would be better off without having to deal with me.\n\nSo… no, not for two years, not in this way. This feels more recent, but it’s been really intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel worthless or guilty at all?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I might have moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking the words, like “I’m worthless” or “I’m guilty,” but I don’t really have whole days where it’s just gone.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always there underneath. The reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. That near-miss keeps turning into this evidence in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, like I shouldn’t be trusted. And then it doesn’t stay contained to that one thing. It becomes this bigger feeling that I’m just failing people in general, and that everyone would be better off without me around or without me causing problems.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I get days free from it. Maybe brief stretches where I’m not focusing on it as much, but it comes back really easily.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any variation in the intensity of your feelings of worthlessness and guilt over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is some variation, but it never feels like it fully lifts, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume changes.\n\nWhen something reminds me of the near-miss, or when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. Then it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it becomes this whole spiral of, “I’m dangerous,” and “I shouldn’t be trusted,” and “I’m not meant to be a nurse.” It feels like that one incident is proof of something terrible about me, even though part of me knows people might say it doesn’t mean that.\n\nAnd then at other times, if I’m distracted or doing something else, it can fade into the background a little. But it’s still there. It’s like I’m carrying around this constant feeling that I’m a disappointment, and that everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about me. So the intensity changes, yes, but the feeling itself doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific events or situations that trigger your feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest trigger is anything that brings me back to that near-miss at work. Even if it’s something small, like thinking about being on the floor, or imagining having to make another judgment call, it just sets off this whole chain in my head.\n\nIt starts with, “I made a mistake,” but it doesn’t stay that simple. It turns into, “I’m dangerous,” like I can’t be trusted with people’s care, and then, “I shouldn’t be a nurse at all.” And once I’m there, it kind of spreads beyond work. I start thinking I’m not just bad at my job or unsafe at my job, but that I’m a disappointment as a person.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels really real when it happens. Like that one incident proves something about me. And then I start thinking everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about what I might mess up next. So yes, anything connected to that near-miss or being a nurse or being responsible for someone can make the guilt and worthlessness get really loud.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. Even small decisions can feel weirdly hard, like what to chart first or what task to start with. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any extended period, say several weeks or a month, where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt were completely absent?\n\nPatient: No… not completely absent. I know I kind of already said that, but it’s hard to explain because sometimes it isn’t as loud every second, so part of me wants to say, “Maybe there were breaks,” but when I really think about it, it’s still there.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps proving, over and over in my head, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. Even if I’m doing something else or I look okay from the outside, that feeling is still underneath it, like… “You shouldn’t be trusted. Everyone would be better off if you weren’t in the way.”\n\nSo no, I haven’t had several weeks or a month where that was just gone. Maybe moments where I’m distracted, but not a real stretch where I felt free of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… nearly every day. Honestly, it feels like every day, even if some parts of the day are quieter than others.

I came in because I just can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Over the past two years… I don’t know that I can honestly say it’s been that long in this exact way. The really intense guilt and worthlessness I’m talking about now feels tied to that near-miss, like it became this thing my brain keeps using as proof that I’m dangerous or that I shouldn’t be a nurse.

But lately, yes, it’s most of the day. I wake up and it’s almost immediately there, like this heavy thought of, “You’re a disappointment, you can’t be trusted, you could hurt someone.” And even if I try to push it aside or do what I’m supposed to do, it comes back over and over. It’s not just feeling bad about a mistake — it feels like I am the mistake, if that makes sense.

I keep thinking everyone would be better off without me being in the way, especially in nursing. Like one near-miss means I’ve already shown who I am, and I can’t stop replaying that. So… for two years, I’m not sure. But recently, it has been most of every day.

It feels like I’m a disappointment—to the people I work with, to patients, to my family, to everyone who thought I could do this. I keep thinking everyone would be better off without me around or involved, because I’m just going to let people down or hurt someone. That’s kind of why I’m here. I don’t know how to stop feeling that way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I think. About a year and two months now. At first I thought it was just stress from trying to do everything and prove I could handle it, but it just kind of stayed and got more tied into how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It becomes this feeling that I disappoint everyone, that I’m a burden, that people would be better off if I wasn’t around making things harder or making them worry. I don’t know if there were earlier periods in the year where it was worse than now… I think there were times it was bad, but this is the point where I couldn’t really keep pretending it was normal guilt anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness and guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most of the day, honestly. I wish I could say it just comes in waves and then I’m okay in between, but it’s more like it’s sitting in the background all the time, and then certain things make it louder.\n\nLike with work, or even just thinking about work, that near-miss keeps coming back into my head. I keep replaying it and thinking, if that happened once, what does that say about me? It’s not only “I made a mistake,” it becomes “I am a mistake,” or “I’m a disappointment,” or “everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me.” So… yeah, it’s there for most of the day. Sometimes it eases up a little if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for more than two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been more than two years like this, no. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any extended period, say several weeks or a month, where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt were completely absent?\n\nPatient: No… not completely absent. Like that one near-miss just keeps proving, over and over in my head, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad about myself before, like I think most people do, but not this constant, heavy thing where it feels like it proves something about who I am.\n\nThis really became something I couldn’t get away from after that near-miss. Since then I just keep thinking I’m a danger, like I shouldn’t be trusted, like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I feel like I’m just a disappointment in general, not just at work. Like everyone would be better off without having to deal with me.\n\nSo… no, not for two years, not in this way. This feels more recent, but it’s been really intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel worthless or guilty at all?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I might have moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking the words, like “I’m worthless” or “I’m guilty,” but I don’t really have whole days where it’s just gone.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always there underneath. The reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. That near-miss keeps turning into this evidence in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, like I shouldn’t be trusted. And then it doesn’t stay contained to that one thing. It becomes this bigger feeling that I’m just failing people in general, and that everyone would be better off without me around or without me causing problems.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I get days free from it. Maybe brief stretches where I’m not focusing on it as much, but it comes back really easily.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any variation in the intensity of your feelings of worthlessness and guilt over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is some variation, but it never feels like it fully lifts, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume changes.\n\nWhen something reminds me of the near-miss, or when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. Then it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it becomes this whole spiral of, “I’m dangerous,” and “I shouldn’t be trusted,” and “I’m not meant to be a nurse.” It feels like that one incident is proof of something terrible about me, even though part of me knows people might say it doesn’t mean that.\n\nAnd then at other times, if I’m distracted or doing something else, it can fade into the background a little. But it’s still there. It’s like I’m carrying around this constant feeling that I’m a disappointment, and that everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about me. So the intensity changes, yes, but the feeling itself doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific events or situations that trigger your feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest trigger is anything that brings me back to that near-miss at work. Even if it’s something small, like thinking about being on the floor, or imagining having to make another judgment call, it just sets off this whole chain in my head.\n\nIt starts with, “I made a mistake,” but it doesn’t stay that simple. It turns into, “I’m dangerous,” like I can’t be trusted with people’s care, and then, “I shouldn’t be a nurse at all.” And once I’m there, it kind of spreads beyond work. I start thinking I’m not just bad at my job or unsafe at my job, but that I’m a disappointment as a person.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels really real when it happens. Like that one incident proves something about me. And then I start thinking everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about what I might mess up next. So yes, anything connected to that near-miss or being a nurse or being responsible for someone can make the guilt and worthlessness get really loud.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. No… I don’t think there’s been an extended period where I felt completely free of it and then it came back. It’s more like it changes volume.

There are moments where I’m distracted or I’m talking to someone and I can almost act normal, but it’s not like the guilt is gone. It’s still underneath everything. I keep coming back to the same thought, that I’m a danger and a disappointment, and that the near-miss means something about me as a person… like it proves I’m not meant to be a nurse.

So I wouldn’t describe it as episodes where it fully goes away. It’s more that sometimes I can push it down for a little while, and then it’s right there again, telling me I shouldn’t be trusted and that everyone would be better off without me in the way.

But the thoughts are there more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be lying there at night or getting ready for work and I’ll think, it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not in a dramatic way, I guess, more like this exhausted thought that if I could just stop existing for a while, or disappear, then I wouldn’t have to keep worrying that I’m going to mess something up again. Or disappoint everyone again. Sometimes it feels like people would be safer or less burdened if I just wasn’t around to cause problems.\n\nI know that sounds bad when I say it out loud. I don’t want to scare anyone, and I’m not saying I’m about to do something. It’s just… those thoughts keep coming up, and they feel really hard to push away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just don't feel pleasure from them even if you try to do them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but maybe it starts with not being able to care enough to even begin. Like, I used to have this whole routine in my head after class or work — I’d go for a jog to clear everything out, or I’d knit because it kept my hands busy and felt calming, or I’d cook something that took a little effort because that used to feel satisfying. Or I’d read, just to be somewhere else for a while.\n\nNow I’ll come home still in my scrubs and just sit on my bed. And I can see the things right there, like my running shoes or my knitting stuff, and I’ll think, I should do that, it used to help. But it feels like there’s no pull toward it anymore. Like I can’t make myself care enough to get up.\n\nAnd if I do try, it’s not really enjoyable. I’m just going through the motions and waiting for it to feel like it used to, and it doesn’t. So then I stop, and I end up back on my bed feeling stupid for even trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past year where your feelings of worthlessness and guilt were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, but it’s hard to compare it neatly, because right now it feels like it’s kind of the worst it’s been. Or at least the most stuck I’ve felt in it.\n\nThe reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps replaying in my head, and it feels like proof, somehow, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. I know maybe people would say, you know, everyone makes mistakes or it was caught, but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like this big flashing sign that I shouldn’t be trusted, and that I’m going to hurt someone if I keep trying to do this.\n\nAnd then it spreads out from there, like it’s not just about work. It becomes this feeling that I disappoint everyone, that I’m a burden, that people would be better off if I wasn’t around making things harder or making them worry. I don’t know if there were earlier periods in the year where it was worse than now… I think there were times it was bad, but this is the point where I couldn’t really keep pretending it was normal guilt anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness and guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most of the day, honestly. I wish I could say it just comes in waves and then I’m okay in between, but it’s more like it’s sitting in the background all the time, and then certain things make it louder.\n\nLike with work, or even just thinking about work, that near-miss keeps coming back into my head. I keep replaying it and thinking, if that happened once, what does that say about me? And then it turns into this whole thing where I’m convinced I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted with people. Even if I’m trying to do something else, it’s like part of me is still stuck on that thought.\n\nAnd then it spreads beyond that. It’s not only “I made a mistake,” it becomes “I am a mistake,” or “I’m a disappointment,” or “everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me.” So… yeah, it’s there for most of the day. Sometimes it eases up a little if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for more than two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been more than two years like this, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad about myself before, like I think most people do, but not this constant, heavy thing where it feels like it proves something about who I am.\n\nThis really became something I couldn’t get away from after that near-miss. Since then I just keep thinking I’m a danger, like I shouldn’t be trusted, like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I feel like I’m just a disappointment in general, not just at work. Like everyone would be better off without having to deal with me.\n\nSo… no, not for two years, not in this way. This feels more recent, but it’s been really intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel worthless or guilty at all?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I might have moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking the words, like “I’m worthless” or “I’m guilty,” but I don’t really have whole days where it’s just gone.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always there underneath. The reason I came in is because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. That near-miss keeps turning into this evidence in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, like I shouldn’t be trusted. And then it doesn’t stay contained to that one thing. It becomes this bigger feeling that I’m just failing people in general, and that everyone would be better off without me around or without me causing problems.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I get days free from it. Maybe brief stretches where I’m not focusing on it as much, but it comes back really easily.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any variation in the intensity of your feelings of worthlessness and guilt over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is some variation, but it never feels like it fully lifts, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume changes.\n\nWhen something reminds me of the near-miss, or when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. Then it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it becomes this whole spiral of, “I’m dangerous,” and “I shouldn’t be trusted,” and “I’m not meant to be a nurse.” It feels like that one incident is proof of something terrible about me, even though part of me knows people might say it doesn’t mean that.\n\nAnd then at other times, if I’m distracted or doing something else, it can fade into the background a little. But it’s still there. It’s like I’m carrying around this constant feeling that I’m a disappointment, and that everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about me. So the intensity changes, yes, but the feeling itself doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific events or situations that trigger your feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest trigger is anything that brings me back to that near-miss at work. Even if it’s something small, like thinking about being on the floor, or imagining having to make another judgment call, it just sets off this whole chain in my head.\n\nIt starts with, “I made a mistake,” but it doesn’t stay that simple. It turns into, “I’m dangerous,” like I can’t be trusted with people’s care, and then, “I shouldn’t be a nurse at all.” And once I’m there, it kind of spreads beyond work. I start thinking I’m not just bad at my job or unsafe at my job, but that I’m a disappointment as a person.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but it feels really real when it happens. Like that one incident proves something about me. And then I start thinking everyone would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me or worry about what I might mess up next. So yes, anything connected to that near-miss or being a nurse or being responsible for someone can make the guilt and worthlessness get really loud.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It feels like it’s been forever sometimes, because it’s so constant, but if I actually count it out, it’s been more like a little over a year.\n\nI think it started after I graduated high school and started trying to do college classes while also working full-time at the clinic. That’s when it really began settling in, the feeling that I wasn’t doing enough or that I was already failing somehow. So… maybe about a year and two months now? Around there. Not two years or more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt go away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, like if I’m distracted or someone is talking to me and I have to focus on something else. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. Even small decisions can feel weirdly hard, like what to chart first or what task to start with. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any extended period, say several weeks or a month, where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt were completely absent?\n\nPatient: No… not completely absent. I know I kind of already said that, but it’s hard to explain because sometimes it isn’t as loud every second, so part of me wants to say, “Maybe there were breaks,” but when I really think about it, it’s still there.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps proving, over and over in my head, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. Even if I’m doing something else or I look okay from the outside, that feeling is still underneath it, like… “You shouldn’t be trusted. Everyone would be better off if you weren’t in the way.”\n\nSo no, I haven’t had several weeks or a month where that was just gone. Maybe moments where I’m distracted, but not a real stretch where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. Honestly, it feels like every day, even if some parts of the day are quieter than others.\n\nI came in because I just can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. It’s like that one near-miss keeps turning into this proof in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and once that thought starts, it just sticks to everything. Even if I’m not actively crying or talking about it, it’s still there underneath, like this constant voice saying I shouldn’t be trusted and that everyone would be better off if I wasn’t in the way.\n\nSo… yes. It’s definitely been more than two weeks. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for most of every day over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Over the past two years… I don’t know that I can honestly say it’s been that long in this exact way. The really intense guilt and worthlessness I’m talking about now feels tied to that near-miss, like it became this thing my brain keeps using as proof that I’m dangerous or that I shouldn’t be a nurse.\n\nBut lately, yes, it’s most of the day. I wake up and it’s almost immediately there, like this heavy thought of, “You’re a disappointment, you can’t be trusted, you could hurt someone.” And even if I try to push it aside or do what I’m supposed to do, it comes back over and over. It’s not just feeling bad about a mistake — it feels like I am the mistake, if that makes sense.\n\nI keep thinking everyone would be better off without me being in the way, especially in nursing. Like one near-miss means I’ve already shown who I am, and I can’t stop replaying that. So… for two years, I’m not sure. But recently, it has been most of every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you felt completely free of worthlessness and guilt for an extended period, followed by a return of these feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been an extended period where I felt completely free of it and then it came back. It’s more like it changes volume.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or I’m talking to someone and I can almost act normal, but it’s not like the guilt is gone. It’s still underneath everything. But I wouldn’t say it goes away completely.\n\nIt’s more like it’s always sitting there underneath everything. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment, like that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse. And when it comes back up, it feels so strong, like it explains everything about me. Like, “See, this is who you are. You shouldn’t be trusted.” \n\nSo I don’t really have clean breaks from it, where I feel okay and then later it returns. It’s more that it gets quieter or louder. Sometimes it’s in the background, but it still feels true, if that makes sense. And then when something reminds me of work or that incident, it just rushes back in and feels overwhelming again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I can’t really point to a two-month stretch where I felt free of it, like where I wasn’t carrying that “I’m a danger” feeling or the guilt about what happened.\n\nThere might be hours, maybe even parts of a day, where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face. But it’s still kind of there, underneath. Like I came in because I can’t stop feeling like that near-miss proves something about me—that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I’m a disappointment, that people would be better off without me causing problems or making mistakes.\n\nSo no, not two months. I wish I could say there was a break like that, but it hasn’t felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of worthlessness and guilt have changed at all since they started, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: I think they’ve gotten worse… or maybe more fixed, if that makes sense. At first it felt more like I had messed up, like I was scared and ashamed about that one near-miss and I kept replaying it. But over time it started feeling less like “I made a mistake” and more like “this proves something is wrong with me.”\n\nNow it’s like I can’t separate myself from it. I keep thinking I’m a danger, like I’m going to hurt someone just by being in the role, and that I’m a disappointment for even trying. It’s not just guilt about what happened anymore. It’s this constant feeling that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone would honestly be better off if I wasn’t in the way or trusted with anything important.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s stayed the same. It’s gotten heavier. It feels more personal now, like it’s about who I am, not just what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate on your daily tasks due to these feelings of worthlessness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like my brain gets thick or foggy when those thoughts are loud. I’ll be trying to do something simple, something I should be able to do, and I just… can’t hold it in my head properly.\n\nLike with medication orders, I’ll read the same line over and over, and I know the words are there, but they don’t really sink in. Then I start thinking, “What if I miss something? What if this proves I shouldn’t be doing this?” and then I have to read it again because now I’m even less sure I understood it. It becomes this loop where the guilt makes me double-check, but the more I double-check, the more frozen I feel.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and reread the same paragraph several times, and I still couldn’t tell you what I just read because part of my mind is off somewhere telling me I’m not good enough or I’m unsafe. Even small decisions can feel weirdly hard, like what to chart first or what task to start with. I’ll just sit there stuck, knowing I need to move, but it feels like my brain won’t pick a direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any extended period, say several weeks or a month, where these feelings of worthlessness and guilt were completely absent?\n\nPatient: No… not completely absent. I know I kind of already said that, but it’s hard to explain because sometimes it isn’t as loud every second, so part of me wants to say, “Maybe there were breaks,” but when I really think about it, it’s still there.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. Like that one near-miss just keeps proving, over and over in my head, that I’m not meant to be a nurse. Even if I’m doing something else or I look okay from the outside, that feeling is still underneath it, like… “You shouldn’t be trusted. Everyone would be better off if you weren’t in the way.”\n\nSo no, I haven’t had several weeks or a month where that was just gone. Maybe moments where I’m distracted, but not a real stretch where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness and guilt been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. Honestly, it feels like every day, even if some parts of the day are quieter than others.\n\nI came in because I just can’t stop feeling like I’m a danger and a disappointment. It’s like that one near-miss keeps turning into this proof in my head that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and once that thought starts, it just sticks to everything. Even if I’m not actively crying or talking about it, it’s still there underneath, like this constant voice saying I shouldn’t be trusted and that everyone would be better off if I wasn’t in the way.\n\nSo… yes. It’s definitely been more than two weeks. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty for most of every day over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Over the past two years… I don’t know that I can honestly say it’s been that long in this exact way. The really intense guilt and worthlessness I’m talking about now feels tied to that near-miss, like it became this thing my brain keeps using as proof that I’m dangerous or that I shouldn’t be a nurse.\n\nBut lately, yes, it’s most of the day. I wake up and it’s almost immediately there, like this heavy thought of, “You’re a disappointment, you can’t be trusted, you could hurt someone.” And even if I try to push it aside or do what I’m supposed to do, it comes back over and over. It’s not just feeling bad about a mistake — it feels like I am the mistake, if that makes sense.\n\nI keep thinking everyone would be better off without me being in the way, especially in nursing. Like one near-miss means I’ve already shown who I am, and I can’t stop replaying that. So… for two years, I’m not sure. But recently, it has been most of every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you felt completely free of worthlessness and guilt for an extended period, followed by a return of these feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been an extended period where I felt completely free of it and then it came back. It’s more like it changes volume.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or I’m talking to someone and I can almost act normal, but it’s not like the guilt is gone. It’s still underneath everything. I keep coming back to the same thought, that I’m a danger and a disappointment, and that the near-miss means something about me as a person… like it proves I’m not meant to be a nurse.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as episodes where it fully goes away. It’s more that sometimes I can push it down for a little while, and then it’s right there again, telling me I shouldn’t be trusted and that everyone would be better off without me in the way."
